a:T48e,Instead of only comparing four binary transitions, AdamW-to-AdamW, AdamW-to-Muon, Muon-to-AdamW, and Muon-to-Muon, the paper constructs mixed optimizers. At each update step, it samples a Muon update with probability p and an AdamW update otherwise, with p in 0.0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 0.9, and 1.0. This turns optimizer identity into a continuous axis and enables a p_PT by p_SFT grid. The score in each cell is decomposed into a global mean, a pretraining row effect, an SFT column effect, and a residual interaction term. If matching optimizers had an intrinsic benefit, the residuals should be systematically better along the p_PT = p_SFT diagonal; the experiments do not show a strong pattern of that kind. The continued-pretraining experiments use the same logic and show that apparent switching costs largely disappear after learning-rate tuning. AdamW uses decoupled weight decay and gradient clipping. Muon follows the paper’s Algorithm 2 and uses the 0.2 RMS update scaling from prior Muon work. The authors also use the expected update RMS for AdamW with beta1 = 0.9 to align update scales and allow a common base learning rate across mixture settings.b:T45f,The main controlled experiments train 1.2B-parameter Llama-3-style models from scratch, with 32 layers, 24 attention heads, full multi-head attention, and hidden size 1536. Pretraining is run at 1x and 8x Chinchilla scales, corresponding to roughly 24B and 193B tokens, using the data mixture from Wen et al. 2026. SFT uses the Tulu-3 mixture for two epochs. Sequences are packed to 4096 tokens for the 1.2B models and Llama-2-7B, and to 8192 tokens for Qwen models. Evaluation covers MMLU, BBH, GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, and MBPP. The 1x Chinchilla PT-SFT optimizer grid is evaluated with three random seeds per pairing. The main empirical findings are that increasing the fraction of Muon updates during SFT generally improves downstream performance regardless of the pretraining optimizer, optimizer-matching residual gains are small, and optimizer consistency reduces pretraining-distribution forgetting without producing a corresponding downstream advantage. The paper also fine-tunes AdamW-pretrained open-weight Llama and Qwen models up to 14B parameters and finds Muon competitive with AdamW for task-general SFT.c:Ta2b,技术路线可以概括成一句话：用 streaming k-PCA 持续追踪梯度主子空间，并把上一时刻的子空间和优化器状态平滑迁移到下一时刻，而不是周期性从零开始重估。

先看问题设定。对某个权重矩阵层，梯度 g_t 是高维的。GaLore 类方法会维护一个低秩基 U_t，把梯度投影到这个 rank-k 子空间里，在低维空间中做 Adam 类更新，再映射回原空间。难点在于 U_t 需要跟着训练变化。GaLore 的做法是隔若干步做一次 SVD 重算，这样每次更新 projector 都像一次 cold restart：旧子空间信息被丢掉，Adam 一阶二阶矩也很难自然继承。OSD 则通过额外优化过程去适配移动子空间，但实现更重，也更难稳定。

Ori 的核心判断是：如果 U_t 与 U_{t-Δ} 在小 Δ 下通常很接近，那么最合理的做法是 warm-start tracking。具体实现上，作者采用受 Oja++ 启发的 streaming k-PCA 更新。每一步都用当前梯度对已有基做轻量旋转，使基逐步贴近新的主方向。其单步代价是 O(dk^2)，相比频繁做全量 SVD 要轻得多，因而可以支持高频甚至 gap-free 的 projector 更新。

但仅仅更新基还不够。低秩优化器真正容易出问题的地方，是基变了以后 optimizer state 怎么办。Ori 在这里加入 drift-aware bootstrapping：把上一时刻子空间基作为当前初始化，并对 Adam 风格的一阶矩、二阶矩做跨基转移。这样做的直觉是，如果子空间只是小角度旋转，那么旧统计量并没有失效，直接丢弃会造成不必要的优化震荡。文中的 drift coefficient ρ 用来控制 carry-over 强度，实验显示 ρ≈0.99 较好，说明“保留连续性但不过度僵化”是有效区间。

从算法结构看，Ori 不是把低秩空间当成静态压缩容器，而是把它当成一个随训练动态演化的状态变量。这个视角与 GaLore 的周期重算有本质区别：GaLore 更像 snapshot estimation，Ori 更像 online tracking。与 OSD 相比，Ori 不依赖额外的非凸追赶过程，而是直接利用 streaming PCA 的结构更新，因此计算和实现都更干净。

理论部分给出了带 drift 的 dynamic regret 分析。作者证明在 noiseless setting 下可得到 sublinear dynamic regret，且对相邻时刻子空间漂移量的依赖较温和。这个理论不是全文最重要的部分，但它至少说明 Ori 的设计不是纯经验拼装：当子空间连续漂移时，在线跟踪而非反复冷启动，确实能得到更合理的误差累积性质。d:T4d6,This is directly relevant to pretraining. First, it reframes low-rank optimization as a training-dynamics problem rather than a pure compression trick: the bottleneck is not only low rank, but stale projectors. Second, it gives a practical answer for memory-constrained pretraining: if the gradient subspace drifts slowly, then frequent, lightweight, continuous updates are better matched to the dynamics than infrequent, heavy recomputation. Third, the gains appear in the very low-rank regime, which is where these methods are actually hard. At generous ranks many approaches work reasonably well; the real test is when each projector dimension is expensive. On 1B LLaMA pretraining, Ori reduces the gap to full-rank Adam by up to 69.3% under a rank-128 optimizer state versus embedding dimension 2048, while remaining compute-efficient. That makes the contribution operational, not merely conceptual.

The contribution spans three layers: an empirical characterization of gradient-subspace drift, a new optimizer design, and drift-aware theoretical guarantees. It does not replace mainstream full-rank training, but it is highly relevant for memory-limited pretraining, continual training, and the broader study of low-rank optimizers.e:Tb1b,The method can be summarized as follows: continuously track the principal gradient subspace with streaming k-PCA, and smoothly transfer both the basis and optimizer statistics across time instead of repeatedly re-estimating the subspace from scratch.

Consider a weight matrix whose gradient at step t is high-dimensional. Gradient-subspace methods maintain a rank-k basis U_t, project the gradient into that subspace, perform an Adam-like update in low dimension, and map the update back to the full space. The hard part is keeping U_t aligned with the current gradient geometry. GaLore handles this by periodic SVD refreshes, which behave like cold restarts: previous subspace information is discarded, and optimizer moments do not naturally carry over. OSD moves toward continuous adaptation but does so through an additional non-convex optimization loop.

Ori starts from a different empirical premise: if U_t is close to U_{t-Δ} for small Δ, then warm-started tracking should be more efficient than repeated re-estimation. The paper instantiates this with a streaming k-PCA update inspired by Oja++. At each step, the current gradient induces a lightweight rotation of the existing basis, nudging it toward the new principal directions. The per-step cost is O(dk^2), much cheaper than frequent full SVD, which makes high-frequency or effectively gap-free projector updates feasible.

A second key piece is optimizer-state continuity. Updating the basis alone is not enough, because low-rank optimizers also maintain first and second moments in the projected space. Ori introduces drift-aware bootstrapping: the previous basis initializes the current one, and Adam-style moments are transferred across successive bases. The intuition is simple: if the subspace only rotates slightly, then old moments are still informative, and resetting them would inject unnecessary optimization noise. The drift coefficient ρ controls how much carry-over is preserved; empirically, a small but strong carry-over around ρ≈0.99 works well.

Conceptually, Ori treats the low-rank subspace as a dynamic state variable that evolves with training, not as a static compression container. That is the main difference from GaLore’s snapshot-style recomputation. Relative to OSD, Ori avoids an extra non-convex catch-up loop and instead uses a structured streaming PCA update, which is cleaner computationally and algorithmically.

The theory section provides dynamic-regret guarantees under drift. In the noiseless setting, the paper derives sublinear dynamic regret with mild dependence on the consecutive drift parameter. This is not the main reason to read the paper, but it does show that the design is not purely heuristic: when the target subspace moves continuously, online tracking has a better cumulative-error story than repeated cold restarts.f:T795,The experiments are organized around two settings: LLaMA-1B pretraining and RoBERTa-Base finetuning.

The pretraining result is the main one. On a 1B LLaMA pretraining task, Ori is tested in a very low-rank regime where the optimizer-state rank is 128 while the model embedding dimension is 2048. This is a stringent setting and a good stress test for projector staleness. The headline result is that Ori reduces the performance gap to full-rank Adam by up to 69.3% relative to prior low-rank baselines. The paper also presents a performance-memory frontier where Ori sits on the favorable lower-left boundary, indicating better accuracy under low memory and low compute.

For finetuning, the paper evaluates RoBERTa-Base across 16 scenarios with even smaller ranks, 8 or 4. Ori outperforms competing methods in 10 of the 16 cases. The gains are less uniformly dominant than in pretraining, which is unsurprising given higher task variability and noisier optimization in finetuning.

The ablations are especially informative. Increasing the projector update period consistently hurts performance, directly quantifying the cost of staleness. This is one of the strongest pieces of evidence in the paper because it isolates the mechanism behind the gains. A second ablation shows that adding a small carry-over, with drift coefficient around ρ≈0.99, improves accuracy, confirming that moment transfer is not a cosmetic detail but a core part of the optimizer. The paper also measures gradient-subspace drift empirically and shows that after warm-up, neighboring subspaces remain close over short windows, which supports the streaming-tracking premise.

From a pretraining-engineering perspective, the most useful takeaways are not just the benchmark wins but three empirical rules: gradient subspaces drift slowly, stale projectors are especially costly at low rank, and preserving optimizer-state continuity is better than repeated resets.10:T698,This one is worth a careful read. A good order is: phenomenon, algorithm, ablations, then theory.

Start with the introduction and the beginning of the optimizer section. The first thing to verify is whether the paper really establishes that gradient-subspace drift is slow and stable rather than noisy and discontinuous. The whole method depends on that claim. Then read the algorithmic core: the modified Oja++ or streaming k-PCA update, how the projector is refreshed at high frequency, and how first and second moments are transferred across successive bases. While reading, keep asking two questions: why is periodic SVD the wrong abstraction here, and why is moment transfer justified instead of dangerous?

In the experiments, prioritize the LLaMA-1B pretraining table and the performance-memory figure, then the ablations on update period and drift coefficient ρ. Those sections make the mechanism most visible. If time is limited, the theory can be deferred. If you work on optimizers, the dynamic-regret section is still worth reading because it formalizes the slow-drift tracking view.

The figures and tables most worth studying are the subspace-drift measurements, the performance-memory frontier, the comparisons against GaLore, OSD, LoRA, and full-rank Adam at different ranks, and the ablations on update frequency and carry-over strength. Expected reading time is 60–90 minutes. Good follow-up questions are whether Oja-style tracking remains stable under noisier gradients, whether moment transfer accumulates mismatch over very long runs, and whether the slow-drift assumption still holds for larger models, longer token budgets, MoE training, or changing curricula.11:T631,Muon can be interpreted as normalized steepest descent under a spectral-norm geometry, implemented through a linear minimization oracle over a spectral-norm ball. BMuon modifies that oracle. For hidden channel j in block ell, it couples the corresponding row of the MLP up-projection with the corresponding column of the down-projection, and assigns an allocation μ_j^ell in [0,1]. All such channels across all MLP blocks share a single budget K, with sum_{ell,j} μ_j^ell = K. For a fixed allocation, BMuon inserts a diagonal reweighting into Muon’s spectral-norm constraint. The allocation is then chosen globally to maximize first-order loss decrease, so channels across layers compete for the same budget rather than being controlled by fixed layerwise sparsity targets. The paper shows that the resulting oracle is exactly normalized steepest descent under a norm Ω_{α,K}. The parameter α controls the strength of channel reweighting, and K controls the global channel budget. The construction recovers Muon when α=0 or K=N. At α=1, the unit ball becomes the convex hull of operator-norm-bounded updates supported on at most K coupled hidden channels. At α=K=1, it reduces to an l1-style aggregation over channel groups. This differs from NuMuon: NuMuon targets low-rank compressibility through a nuclear-norm constraint, while BMuon targets structured hidden-channel compressibility. Since exact allocation would be expensive at every step, the practical algorithm uses a minorization-maximization surrogate for the allocation update, followed by a weighted Muon update.12:T442,Its relevance to pretraining is mainly through training mechanics rather than language modeling objectives. The contribution is not a new LM loss, but a different answer to the memory wall: change the gradient pathway itself instead of only compressing or recomputing activations. That matters for three reasons. First, it pushes local learning from a mostly empirical idea toward a gradient-estimation framework with theory, at least for deep linear networks and residual nonlinear networks near the right regime. Second, the locality definition is operationally meaningful for large-model training: only one block’s activations need to be kept at a time. Third, the LLM finetuning results suggest that first-order local gradient surrogates can beat zeroth-order methods under similar memory budgets, which is a useful signal for low-memory adaptation. The limitation is equally important: the evidence is not yet large-scale from-scratch LLM pretraining. So this is better viewed as a promising training paradigm candidate than as a drop-in replacement for standard pretraining recipes.13:T77d,The method, ProxyProp, partitions a deep transformer into gradient-isolated blocks. For block l, instead of waiting for gradients to flow through all downstream blocks, the method attaches a local proxy network that takes the block output h_l and predicts the final network output. The proxy is trained by distillation against the global model output. The block update is then computed by backpropagating through the proxy, not through the actual downstream stack.

The central idea is not merely to add an auxiliary head. The proxy is meant to estimate the downstream input-output Jacobian seen from that block. In deep linear networks, the paper argues that an optimal linear proxy implicitly recovers the expected linear map from the current representation to the final output, making the local gradient exact. For nonlinear residual networks, the analysis depends on residual parameterization: when skip connections dominate, the downstream mapping is close to identity plus a small perturbation, so the proxy-induced gradient error can be bounded; at initialization, the local gradient can even match the true gradient exactly. This is the main reason the method is positioned for modern residual transformers rather than arbitrary deep architectures.

Operationally, each block can be updated immediately after its forward pass, satisfying the decoupled or unlocked learning setup. Memory savings come from avoiding full-network activation storage and retaining only the local computation graph for one block plus its proxy. Compared with classic local-loss training, ProxyProp is trying to predict the effect of the remaining network on the final output, rather than forcing each block to solve the task directly. Compared with zeroth-order methods such as MeZO, it still uses gradient information, but obtains it through a local surrogate, which is why it can be more compute-efficient at similar memory cost.14:T672,The experiments appear to have two main parts. The first is supervised learning with vision transformers, used as a controlled setting to study the memory-accuracy trade-off under different block partitions. The reported takeaway is that ProxyProp dominates prior local learning baselines across partition choices: for a given memory budget it achieves better accuracy, or for a given accuracy it uses less memory. The important pattern to inspect is how performance degrades as the model is split into more blocks and locality becomes stronger. The claim is that ProxyProp degrades more gracefully, which would support the gradient-approximation story.

The second part is memory-efficient LLM finetuning. Here the comparison to MeZO is the key result. The paper states that ProxyProp achieves better downstream performance at comparable memory cost, even when MeZO is allowed roughly 8x more compute time. That is a meaningful comparison because zeroth-order optimization is often considered when memory is extremely constrained, but it suffers from high variance and slow convergence.

The currently available text does not expose enough full tables to restate every concrete number with confidence, such as exact ViT sizes, datasets, partition counts, or the LLM parameter scales and task suite. When reading the full experimental section, three details are worth checking carefully: whether memory accounting includes optimizer state and proxy overhead, whether runtime is reported as wall-clock or FLOPs, and whether the LLM finetuning updates all parameters or only PEFT-style modules. That last point changes the practical significance a lot.15:T404,For a skim, start with the definitions in the introduction: locality, decoupling, and unlocked training. Those tell you what class of methods the paper is actually competing with. Then go straight to the theory sections on two claims: why the proxy gives exact gradients in deep linear networks, and why the error remains controlled in nonlinear residual networks under residual parameterization and skip-dominant regimes. Those sections carry most of the paper’s intellectual weight.

In the experiments, focus on three items: the accuracy-memory curves as the number of blocks changes, the ablation on proxy design, and the fairness of the LLM finetuning comparison against MeZO. A useful reading question is whether the gains come mainly from a better local target or from enabling a more favorable memory/parallelism regime. That distinction determines whether ProxyProp should be thought of primarily as an optimization method or as a systems-facing training alternative. A solid skim should take around 30 to 45 minutes.16:T603,SPLIT models success as:
P(success) = P(at least one correct candidate exists) × P(it is selected | it exists),
and explicitly models the second term (selection), not just coverage.

Section 3 derives exact success formulas for two selectors widely used:
1) Uniform selection among verifier-accepted candidates.
2) Argmax selection by verifier score.

The formulas incorporate verifier true/false accept rates and three correlation sources observed in practice:
- Heterogeneous task difficulty (per-task solve rates vary widely).
- Shared blind spots across generators, modeled via a Gaussian copula.
- Correlated repeated verifier calls for the same candidate, modeled via a candidate-level acceptance propensity.

Section 4 estimates these ingredients from a small labeled pilot using empirical Bayes, including variants where verifier accuracy depends on task difficulty (to avoid biased pooling).

A planner then searches over configurations—generator choices, sample counts, verifier calls, thresholds—under a cost budget to maximize predicted success. The paper also provides a regret guarantee tied to differential (not absolute) forecast error (Proposition 4), explaining why approximate calibration can still yield near-optimal allocations.

Section 6.3 turns several empirical “folk observations” into testable mechanisms/conditions, including when majority-of-k re-verification reduces attainable precision and why false-positive rates can rise with stronger generators due to threshold calibration in scored verifiers.17:T4df,Across five allocation problems (real or simulated verifiers) plus an agentic threshold-selection setting, SPLIT’s chosen configuration is within 0.02–0.8 percentage points of the hindsight-optimal allocation on held-out tasks.

Fixed heuristics—“use the strongest model,” “sample more than you verify,” “add more judges,” and an adaptation of compute-optimal exponent rules—each lose at least 2.5 points in some realistic setting.

Concrete correlation/pathology evidence reported:
- On MATH, majority voting over Llama-3-8B-Instruct stays around ~43% while coverage reaches ~98%.
- Difficulty heterogeneity: an average 27% solve rate would imply 96% coverage at 10 tries under i.i.d., but measured per-problem rates yield 61%, with 44/128 problems almost never solved.
- Cross-generator correlation: per-problem solve rates of Llama-3-8B-Instruct and Gemma-7B correlate at 0.80.
- Judge correlation: about one-third of wrong MATH-500 answers fool at least 6 of 8 LLM judges.

Engineering/reproducibility: uses publicly released samples/verdicts, re-executes 80,403 CodeContests programs locally, reports paired significance tests, and includes a long-horizon evaluation on SWE-Bench Verified with a pool of 175 agent systems.18:T580,The work first documents three forms of model-specific bias. Generation bias is measured under repeated sampling: when self-consistency fails, wrong samples often collapse onto the same dominant wrong answer rather than spreading across alternatives. The paper quantifies this with C_q, the fraction of incorrect samples assigned to the most frequent wrong answer, under SC@20 on benchmarks such as MMLU-Pro and GPQA-Diamond. Initial-answer persistence bias is measured by asking the model for a direct answer first and then asking it to reason before answering; the post-reasoning answer remains strongly tied to the initial prediction, especially when the initial prediction is wrong. Longer-reasoning preference bias appears in self-evaluation: when the model judges its own earlier and later answers, it overwhelmingly prefers the later, more elaborate one. In disagreement cases, the later-stage answer is selected more than 95% of the time, often with high confidence even when it is wrong. Dia, the proposed method, uses these observations directly. It compares an intermediate-stage prediction with the final-stage prediction. If they agree, the final answer is returned. If they disagree, a small heterogeneous solver panel answers the question independently without seeing either candidate. The final decision is made by answer-level aggregation rather than by a judge reading candidate rationales.19:T43f,The theory starts from a fixed window W. Each agent first pays for the question and peer messages, denoted H, plus a small fixed overhead c. The remaining reasoning budget is g = W - H - c. Instead of assigning each agent a fixed probability of being correct, the paper models ability as a function of g: too little budget prevents the agent from reaching an answer, more budget improves accuracy, and the gains eventually saturate. This ability curve is fitted with a thinking-gain function φ using held-out calibration data. Votes are then aggregated with a Condorcet jury model that permits correlated errors. The framework defines a per-answer margin δ, group accuracy A(n), an effective sample size n_eff, and a synergy index S(n)=δ√n_eff. With single-agent cap sweeps and an initial estimate of error correlation, the model predicts an interior optimal debate size n* in closed form, without running a full grid over debate configurations. The same accounting extends to heterogeneous model sets, where the rule selects both how many models to include and which models to use.1a:T51a,The controlled dense experiments use looped models with 730M stored parameters and 2.9B unrolled parameter applications. Each looped model stores 28 Transformer blocks but executes a logical path of 112 block applications. The Ouro-style baseline is R28^4: a 28-block stack repeated four times. The Huginn-style model is P8 R12^8 C8: an 8-layer prelude, a 12-layer tied recurrent core repeated eight times, and an 8-layer coda, again totaling 112 logical block executions. Two feedforward anchors are used: D28, matching resident parameters, and D112, matching logical depth with independent parameters. The ablations show that the sandwich envelope gives the clearest gains, especially on math, context extraction, and reasoning-heavy tasks. Input injection improves knowledge retrieval and prompt-context utilization, but can hurt some quantitative reasoning. Random initialization is not consistently useful, and separate high/low recurrent states add complexity without dependable improvements. The full Huginn design outperforms Ouro on all ten dense benchmarks. In MoE experiments matched by resident and active parameters, Huginn again beats Ouro, balances expert load better, uses different experts across loop iterations, and approaches a larger feedforward MoE while storing far fewer parameters.1b:Tca2,论文先给理论分析，再给 LED 机制。

第一部分是对 MDM 与 UDM 的优化差异做 ELBO 分解。沿用 path-space ELBO 的写法，目标可拆成 transition prediction loss 和 schedule matching loss。标准 MDM 的 forward transition 是朝 mask token 的吸收过程，schedule 与 denoiser 无关，因此 schedule matching 项为零；模型只需学好反向预测。UDM 的 forward/reverse 过程则涉及全词表上的替换，等价地会引入一个依赖 denoiser 的 schedule matching 项。这个项不是常数，而是模型相关的额外误差源，所以 UDM 的 excess loss 更大，也更难优化。这个分析给出了“为什么 MDM 往往更稳”的明确理由，而不是经验判断。

第二部分是表达能力分析。作者用 circuit complexity 来刻画并行模型的计算深度。结论是：即便允许 arbitrary-order prediction 和双向注意力，标准 MDM 仍停留在 TC0 这一常数深度并行计算类里，和 AR 模型在复杂度层级上相近；UDM 因为允许 rewritable state，可以通过多轮重写实现更深的层级计算，达到 TC0 并支持 NC1 级别的层次化计算，例如求值嵌套布尔表达式。这里的重点不是复杂度标签本身，而是“可重写中间状态”带来了真正不同的计算机制。

LED 的设计正是围绕这个结论展开。它不改 MDM 的 commit 机制，而是额外引入一个 proposal branch。训练时，先用一个预训练 MDM 生成 proposal sequence，再把 proposal 作为条件输入，训练 LED 模型用标准交叉熵去预测目标 token。由于 proposal 里既有对的 token，也有错的 token，模型被迫学会两件事：什么时候保留 proposal，什么时候编辑 proposal。这里没有引入 UDM 那种模型依赖的 diffusion schedule，仍然保持 MDM 风格的简单监督。

推理时，LED 在每个 commit stage 内部交替执行 proposal stage 和 commit stage。proposal stage 维护一个可重写的中间状态，允许 token 在多个 lookahead steps 中被反复生成和修正；commit stage 则遵循标准 masked diffusion，一旦某个 token 被提交，就在后续 stage 中固定不再改写。这样，rewritability 被限制在 proposal 分支内部，而最终输出路径仍然是 MDM 式的逐步提交。跨 stage 时，已经验证较可靠的 proposal token 会被保留，错误 token 则在后续 proposal 中继续修正。

这个结构的关键取舍很清楚。为什么不直接训练 UDM？因为那会把 rewritability 放进主生成过程，训练目标更难。为什么不只做普通 MDM？因为 commit-once 机制限制了迭代修正。LED 选择把“可改写”放在辅助分支，把“可训练”留在主分支。它更像是在 MDM 外面包了一层 lookahead editor，而不是把整个模型改成 uniform diffusion。

从实现角度看，LED 也不是简单 reranking。proposal branch 不是只给一个草稿，而是在每个 commit stage 内部多步迭代，形成真正的中间可编辑状态；commit branch 则利用这个 refined proposal 作为额外条件，决定哪些 token 现在值得锁定。这个 proposal/commit 解耦，是全文最值得反复琢磨的设计点。1c:Tcee,The paper first develops the theory, then introduces LED.

The optimization analysis starts from the path-space ELBO for discrete diffusion. The objective can be decomposed into a transition-prediction term and a schedule-matching term. In standard MDMs, the forward process is an absorbing transition toward a mask token, and the schedule is independent of the denoiser, so the schedule-matching term vanishes. The model only needs to learn reverse prediction. In UDMs, transitions operate over the full vocabulary, and the schedule effectively depends on the denoiser, introducing an extra model-dependent schedule-matching loss. This creates additional excess loss and makes optimization harder. The paper therefore gives a principled reason, not just an empirical one, for why MDMs tend to train more easily.

The expressivity analysis uses circuit complexity. The key result is that standard MDMs remain in TC0, even with arbitrary-order prediction and bidirectional attention. In other words, their parallel computational depth stays constant because committed tokens cannot be rewritten. UDMs, by contrast, maintain a rewritable state and can repeatedly revise tokens, which enables richer hierarchical computation and supports NC1-style behavior. The exact complexity labels matter less than the underlying mechanism: rewritable intermediate states fundamentally change what the model can compute in parallel.

LED is built around this observation. It keeps the MDM-style commit process intact, but adds a proposal branch that is allowed to rewrite tokens. During training, a pretrained MDM first generates a proposal sequence. LED is then trained, with standard cross-entropy, to predict the target sequence conditioned on that proposal. Because the proposal contains both correct and incorrect tokens, the model must learn when to preserve a proposal token and when to edit it. This preserves the simple supervision of MDMs and avoids introducing a UDM-style model-dependent diffusion schedule.

At inference time, LED alternates between a proposal stage and a commit stage within each commit block. The proposal stage maintains a rewritable intermediate state, allowing tokens to be generated and revised over multiple lookahead steps. The commit stage then follows the standard masked-diffusion process: once a token is committed, it is fixed and cannot be changed later. Across stages, reliable proposal tokens are preserved, while erroneous ones are revised in future proposal iterations.

This is the central design trade-off. Why not train a UDM directly? Because rewritability inside the main generative process makes optimization harder. Why not stay with a plain MDM? Because commit-once decoding limits iterative correction. LED places rewritability in an auxiliary branch and keeps trainability in the main branch. It is better understood as a lookahead editor wrapped around an MDM than as a uniform diffusion model.

Implementation-wise, LED is also more than draft-and-revise reranking. The proposal branch is iterated inside each commit stage, creating a genuine editable latent text state. The commit branch uses the refined proposal as additional conditioning to decide which tokens are safe to lock in. This proposal/commit decoupling is the most important design idea in the paper.1d:T783,The experiments use Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen3-8B as backbones, trained on the same data with either standard MDM SFT or LED SFT. Evaluation covers eight benchmarks across math, code, and science. The headline result is strong and simple: LED SFT improves over standard MDM SFT by +11.4 average points for Qwen2.5-7B and +7.7 points for Qwen3-8B. The paper also reports that LED-8B, trained with SFT alone, outperforms LLaDA2.1-16B Q-Mode, which uses continued pretraining, SFT, and RL. That suggests the gain is not a minor tuning effect but a meaningful shift in effective capability.

The mechanism-facing experiments are especially useful. The paper studies the number of lookahead editing steps, the quality of the proposal sequence, and the interaction between proposal and commit stages. The general pattern is that moderate iterative revision helps, but returns do not grow without bound, which supports the interpretation that the proposal branch provides a revisable intermediate state rather than unlimited extra compute. The paper also shows selective editing behavior: high-quality proposal tokens are often preserved, while incorrect ones are revised. This is evidence that the model is learning to edit, not merely to ignore the proposal.

The complexity discussion is accompanied by synthetic or structured tasks illustrating the benefit of rewritable state for hierarchical computation. Even if those tasks are not the main benchmark story, they help explain why gains are especially visible on math and code, where iterative correction of intermediate structure matters more than one-shot commitment.

From a pretraining and modeling perspective, the most important empirical fact is this: with the same backbone, same data, and same SFT budget, changing the modeling and decoding structure alone yields 7–11 point average gains. That is strong evidence that the expressivity bottleneck of plain MDMs is real.1e:T693,This one deserves a careful read in the order of motivation, mechanism, and evidence.

Start with the introduction and the ELBO analysis. The key question is why the schedule-matching term is zero for MDMs but model-dependent for UDMs. That explains why the paper does not simply move toward a UDM.

Then read the circuit-complexity section. Even if you do not follow every proof, make sure to understand the conclusion: commit-only generation limits computational depth, while rewritable state enables more hierarchical parallel computation. This is the most unusual part of the paper, and also the part that gives the strongest conceptual explanation.

Next, study the LED method figure and the training/inference procedure carefully. You want a precise picture of the proposal stage, commit stage, lookahead steps, and how tokens are preserved or revised across stages. It is easy to misread LED as a simple draft-then-edit system, but the actual design is more structured: an editable proposal branch is maintained inside each commit stage.

In the experiments, prioritize the main table, the comparison against LLaDA2.1-16B Q-Mode, the ablation on lookahead steps, the analysis of proposal quality, and any qualitative or quantitative evidence of selective editing. Those parts tell you where the gains come from.

If time is limited, read the introduction, the theory summary figures, the method figure, the main results table, and the key ablations; that should take around 40–60 minutes. If you plan to build on this line of work, read the appendix as well, especially the complexity proofs, training details, and the implementation of proposal generation and conditioning.1f:T49f,PONDER frames sequential masked generation as an MDP.
- State: the current partially filled canvas x^(t) (visible patches as tokens, missing patches as MASK).
- Frozen generator: a backbone produces per-patch features from x^(t); a diffusion/continuous head fills selected patches.
- Policy actions: choose a set of patches to unmask and generate next; optionally choose previously generated patches to re-mask so they will be regenerated later (“rethinking”). This explicitly models revision rather than hoping later steps fix early mistakes.
- Training: keep the generator fixed; train only the policy with RL using a terminal reward computed on the final sample. Crucially, the method does not need token probabilities or exact log-likelihoods from the generator, which is what makes it applicable beyond discrete masked LMs.
- FIDgrad reward: approximate each sample’s marginal contribution to FID by linearizing the FID objective in Inception-feature mean/covariance statistics, yielding an image-level scalar reward usable by policy gradients.
At inference, a rethinking budget R can be adjusted to trade compute for quality while keeping both generator and policy fixed.20:T89e,方法核心是 Sep-Latent。整体训练是一个从显式 CoT 逐步过渡到隐式推理的 curriculum。作者观察到分隔符 token，例如换行、逗号、句号，在注意力里天然容易成为结构汇聚点，于是把它们当作 semantic anchors。

第一部分是 Attention-Decay。做法是在显式 CoT 路径中，对非分隔符的 step tokens 施加逐步增强的 attention suppression。直观上，模型原本可以直接在每个推理词上保留和传播信息；加入 decay 后，这些普通 token 的可访问信息通路被压窄，模型被迫把关键语义压缩到相邻的 separator hidden states 上。这样，separator 不再只是标点，而变成承载步骤摘要的 latent carrier。这个设计和直接引入额外 latent token 不同，它复用了原始词表中的结构 token，因此不需要额外定义固定长度的隐变量槽位。

第二部分是 dual-path distillation。论文同时保留显式路径和隐式学生路径，并在 token-level 与 sequence-level 做多层蒸馏对齐。显式路径提供可监督的 CoT 轨迹，隐式路径学习用更少的 latent anchors 复现这些推理状态。作者强调 online-policy distillation，即蒸馏对象来自模型自身当前策略生成的 latent trajectory，而不是完全离线的教师缓存。这一设计据称能降低训练内存开销，也让学生更贴近推理时真实会走到的状态分布。

第三部分是 Dynamic Implicit Exit。模型学习一个退出信号，根据当前问题复杂度和内部推理状态决定何时停止 latent reasoning 并输出答案。相比固定 latent 步数，这让简单问题可以更早结束，复杂问题保留更深的内部计算。

从建模角度看，这篇工作的关键不是提出新的 backbone，而是把“结构 token + 注意力约束 + 双路径蒸馏 + 动态退出”组合成一个隐式推理训练框架。它与 Coconut/CODI 的主要区别在于：latent 容量不再由固定数量的专用 latent token 决定，而是由输入中自然出现的 separator 及其压缩后的表示来承载；同时用 attention-decay 主动塑造信息流，而不是只靠蒸馏目标被动学到压缩。21:T77e,Sep-Latent is a curriculum that transitions from explicit CoT to implicit reasoning. The central observation is that separator tokens such as newlines, commas, and periods already act as structural aggregation points in attention. The method amplifies that tendency and turns those tokens into semantic anchors.

The first component is Attention-Decay. During training on the explicit reasoning path, the model progressively suppresses attention to non-separator step tokens. This narrows the information pathways through ordinary reasoning words and forces the model to route and compress useful intermediate information into the hidden states of separator tokens. In effect, separators become compact carriers of step-level reasoning summaries. This differs from methods that introduce a fixed number of dedicated latent tokens: Sep-Latent reuses natural vocabulary items and lets the compression structure emerge around them.

The second component is dual-path distillation. An explicit path with textual CoT is kept as a supervised reference, while an implicit student path learns to reproduce the reasoning trajectory using compressed latent anchors. Distillation is applied at both token and sequence levels. The paper further uses online-policy distillation, meaning the model distills from its own current latent trajectories rather than only from an offline teacher cache. The claimed benefit is lower memory cost and better alignment with the states encountered at inference time.

The third component is Dynamic Implicit Exit. The model predicts when to stop latent reasoning based on the current internal state and the difficulty of the query, instead of consuming a fixed latent budget for every example.

The novelty is therefore not a new backbone, but a training framework that combines structural-token anchoring, constrained information flow, explicit-to-implicit distillation, and adaptive stopping.22:T461,The evaluation covers math and commonsense reasoning benchmarks. The headline number in the provided text is 56.8% on GSM8K using only 5.4 tokens on average, with more than 2x better token efficiency than CODI and SIM-CODI. The paper’s claim is not that it reaches the highest absolute reasoning accuracy, but that it preserves competitive performance with much shorter reasoning traces.

Several supporting observations are reported. Attention-Decay is said to compress reasoning into separator anchors with negligible loss in perplexity and base accuracy. PCA visualizations suggest that the latent representations remain semantically diverse rather than collapsing into a narrow region, and that they stay grounded in the vocabulary space. Dynamic exit allows the amount of implicit computation to vary with task complexity.

From a foundation-model perspective, the experiments are more of a proof of concept than a scaling study. The paper demonstrates feasibility and efficiency gains, but it does not yet establish how robust the method is across larger models, different tokenizers, or broader training regimes.23:T5af,Causal Edit Serialization appends a structured edit stream after the source sequence. Each edit is decomposed into causal decisions: emit an operation token, localize a source position or span, and then emit payload tokens when needed. The supported operations are delete, insert, replace, copy, and move. Programs are delimited by edit-start and edit-end markers, and a boundary marker separates protected prefix from editable suffix. Edits are ordered left to right and refer to original source positions, so addresses do not shift after earlier edits are applied. The main mechanism is a pair of outputs: the vocabulary head selects a pointer role, such as replacement start or insertion location, and a lightweight localization head scores legal preceding source positions. When pointer tokens are fed back, a dual-index RoPE encodes both stream position and referenced source address. This lets the model condition on previously emitted pointers while preserving the usual causal attention mask. Training mixes ordinary language modeling with serialized repairs derived from random corruptions, model-generated edits, and mined copy examples. Compared with numeric-address edit scripts, CES avoids multi-token number generation and parsing. Compared with relative-distance vocabularies, it avoids committing to a fixed distance inventory. Compared with infilling or iterative editing models, it keeps the model in a single decoder-only causal format.24:T660,Two coupled components: a region-inhomogeneous forward process (training) and a detect–remask–refill sampler (inference).

1) Region-inhomogeneous forward dynamics
- Split positions into holes (to be generated) and user-provided context.
- Holes follow standard absorbing corruption over time t.
- Context follows a uniform-mixing corruption kernel at a small per-sequence rate η, which is equivalent to keeping context at a small, fixed “elapsed time” under uniform mixing while holes traverse absorbing dynamics.
- Training remains a masked-diffusion-style weighted cross-entropy denoising objective, but with modified forward marginals.
- Include an η=0 mixture component so the model still trains on perfectly clean conditioning, aiming to prevent degradation on clean inputs.

2) Detect–remask–refill with trust annealing
- Because context is sometimes corrupted during training, the model outputs a meaningful predictive distribution at every position, including the probability of the observed context token—used as a suspicion score.
- During early reverse steps, low-confidence context tokens are re-noised into the absorbing state (remasked) with an annealed intensity, then refilled by the standard reverse dynamics with bidirectional conditioning.
- After a lock time, context is frozen to avoid late-stage instability.
- The paper discusses confidence bookkeeping to compose this with low-confidence decoding.

Conceptually, absorbing diffusion is framed as one endpoint on a “trust axis” (infinite SNR for known regions); the method moves context to finite SNR so the model can doubt and revise it.25:T465,They evaluate corrupted-context infilling on web text with preregistered hypotheses and decision gates, and disentangle training-time vs inference-time contributions via nine experimental arms.
- Diagnostic: under absorbing dynamics, even uniformly random garbage context is treated as highly reliable; the pretrained model assigns on the order of ~50% probability mass to observed context tokens even when they are random, indicating a lack of a graded uncertainty mechanism.
- Results are strongest for conspicuous/uniform corruptions: finetuning under the new forward process yields better detection/repair/containment with no measurable regression under clean conditioning.
- For more plausible corruptions, detection is partial and infilling gains are small; the preregistered primary F1 gate fails.
- Ablations are not fully clean: the paper notes it does not completely isolate the effects of the η=0 mixture and trust annealing from other coupled changes.
- An offered (non-validated) interpretation: detectability and damage correlate with the divergence between the corruption kernel and the true data conditional.26:T6bb,CatchUp is a training-free framework for depth-aware visual-token re-admission. Instead of making a one-shot keep/drop decision, it re-evaluates query-conditioned visual relevance at selected decoder layers. At each routing layer, the model uses attention from the last text token to visual tokens as a relevance proxy, keeps only the selected visual tokens active, and defers the rest rather than deleting them. Between routing layers, only active visual tokens continue through the decoder, reducing attention, FFN, and KV-cache cost.

The key contribution is what happens when a deferred token becomes relevant again. Prior recoverable routing methods simply reinsert the old hidden state, which is stale. The authors first run an oracle analysis with full-token trajectories and show that replacing stale states with same-depth oracle states substantially improves grounding. This establishes that representation staleness is the real bottleneck. CatchUp then approximates the missed evolution using reference-to-current transition signals from semantically and spatially related active visual tokens. Intuitively, it borrows how nearby tokens changed from the reference depth to the current depth, and transfers that change to the returning token. So it does not replay all skipped layers; it estimates the missing state update from related tokens.

Compared with irreversible pruning, CatchUp can recover late-emerging evidence. Compared with recoverable routing without state correction, it explicitly addresses hidden-state mismatch at re-entry. The oracle replacement study is important because it validates the design target: the problem is not re-admission itself, but stale representations at the current depth.27:T614,The main experiments are built on LLaVA-1.5-7B, with referring-expression grounding and general multimodal benchmarks. The motivating analysis is one of the strongest parts of the paper. On 500 RefCOCO samples with 576 visual tokens and top-64 relevance sets, the average adjacent-layer volatility of the top-K visual token set is 28.85%, showing that query-relevant evidence changes substantially across depth. Using layer 2 as the early-selection reference, the late-emerging evidence ratio exceeds 50% by layer 7 and reaches 99.42% at layer 31. In other words, most tokens that matter later would be missed by an early irreversible selection.

On the main benchmarks, CatchUp achieves the best grounding retention among the compared efficient methods under matched routing budgets. Under matched prefill computation, it uses the fewest visual tokens and the smallest decoder KV-cache, while reducing end-to-end latency by 8.0% relative to dense inference. The paper emphasizes that the gain is not from more aggressive pruning alone, but from preserving accuracy better at the same budget.

The most informative ablation is oracle state replacement: if re-admitted tokens are given their full-token same-depth states, grounding performance improves markedly, confirming that stale states are the dominant issue in recoverable routing. Additional ablations indicate that semantic and spatial transition signals are complementary, and that routing frequency matters: too few routing points miss late evidence, while too many reduce the efficiency benefit.28:T7f6,核心思路是把 CODA（训练侧）提出的 GEMM-plus-epilogue 编程范式迁移到推理侧，并针对 prefill 与 decode 的不同约束分别设计。
- Prefill（FP8）路径：
  - 使用 persistent FP8 GEMM，并在 epilogue 里完成 RoPE、SwiGLU、残差更新、缩放/反缩放、RMSNorm 统计收集、以及把 K/V 直接写入 KV-cache（direct KV-cache placement）。
  - “延迟缩放（delayed scaling）”：在 producing epilogue 里直接写出 FP8 值，减少额外的精度转换与中间张量落盘。
  - RMSNorm 融合：推理权重固定，作者把 RMSNorm 的 gain 折叠进下一层投影权重（fold gain into weights），把逐行的 normalization factor 放到该投影的 epilogue 应用，从而避免显式 materialize 归一化后的激活张量。
  - Packed prefill：支持不同 request 长度的 batch，不用 padding，把“无效行”从 GEMM 行维度里剔除。
- Decode（INT4）路径：
  - 目标是减少权重与 KV-cache 的字节读取：投影权重 INT4，KV-cache 也维护 INT4 副本。
  - Hopper 没有 INT4 Tensor Core MMA；因此需要在进入 16-bit MMA 前展开 INT4。关键设计点是“在哪里展开”：
    - 权重：把 nibble 到 FP16/BF16 的转换尽量放在寄存器里，用短逻辑序列与 packed BF16 操作完成；group scales 在累加后再乘，避免 inner loop 里做标量乘。
    - KV-cache：按 MMA fragment 需要的布局存储（fragment-aligned packing），用 byte-indexed lookup 把 packed cache 值转成 FP16 pair。
  - Outlier-aware cache quantization：在量化前对 key 做 block-diagonal Hadamard transform，把大通道 outlier 能量“摊开”，降低 INT4 量化误差；同时对 query 与 attention output 做匹配变换，保证 attention dot product 在变换空间里保持一致（等价变换）。
- 评估与正确性：提供 layer-level 与 end-to-end 的数值误差检查，并用真实 Llama-3.1-8B checkpoint 在 WikiText-2 上跑完整 decode，测 perplexity 与误差累积。29:T7c3,The work ports CODA’s GEMM-plus-epilogue programming model from training to inference, then specializes it for prefill vs decode.
- FP8 prefill path:
  - Persistent FP8 GEMMs with compile-time-selected epilogue policies.
  - Epilogues apply RoPE/SwiGLU, residual updates, scaling/restore, collect RMSNorm statistics, and write K/V directly into the KV cache (direct placement).
  - Delayed scaling: write FP8 outputs directly from the producing epilogue to reduce extra conversions and intermediate materialization.
  - RMSNorm fusion via weight folding: since inference weights are fixed, fold RMSNorm gain into the next projection weights before quantization; apply the per-row normalization factor in that projection’s epilogue, eliminating the explicit normalized activation tensor.
  - Packed prefill: avoid padding for variable-length requests by removing padded rows from the GEMM row dimension.
- INT4 decode path:
  - Use INT4 projection weights and maintain an INT4 copy of the KV cache to reduce bytes read.
  - Hopper lacks native INT4 MMA; INT4 must be expanded before 16-bit MMA. The key design choice is where expansion happens:
    - Weights: nibble-to-FP16/BF16 conversion in registers using short logic sequences and packed BF16 ops; apply group scales after accumulation to keep the inner loop lean.
    - KV cache: fragment-aligned packing (store in the order consumed by MMA fragments) and a small byte-indexed lookup to convert packed cache values into FP16 pairs.
  - Outlier-aware cache quantization: apply a block-diagonal Hadamard transform to keys before quantization to spread channel outliers and reduce INT4 error; apply matching transforms to queries and attention outputs so the attention computation remains equivalent in the transformed space.
- Correctness/evaluation strategy:
  - Layer-level comparisons against PyTorch.
  - End-to-end model evaluation on a real Llama-3.1-8B checkpoint, measuring perplexity and error accumulation across layers.2a:T43c,They propose Uncertainty-Aware Allocation (UAA), separating selection from allocation:
- Value decides which candidate actions to keep.
- Uncertainty decides how many children/rollouts are worth expanding.

Core empirical claim: sibling semantic dispersion at the current step predicts redundancy of downstream continuations, enabling you to set a subtree budget before expanding it.

Key pieces:
- Uncertainty proxy: cosine dispersion/similarity among sibling actions (embedding-based), avoiding auxiliary trained scorers.
- Information-theoretic motivation: interpret per-step budget as Monte Carlo sample size for estimating residual answer entropy; under a Lipschitz assumption, fewer samples suffice when uncertainty is low.
- Two mechanisms: (i) uncertainty-aware pruning (UAP) to keep only a high-quality subset at low-uncertainty steps; (ii) uncertainty-aware budgeting (UAB) to shrink next-step budgets for retained low-uncertainty actions.
- Plug-and-play integration with Beam Search, MCTS, DVTS, REBASE; supports white-box and black-box uncertainty proxies (e.g., GPT-4o).2b:T8d4,作者研究的是 uniform prefill expert reduction：对所有 prompt token，把每层 routed experts 从 K 减到 k'，但生成 token 仍保持完整 K，shared experts 也不减。以 Qwen3-30B-A3B 为主模型，默认 8→4；单 token 干预时用 8→2 以放大效应。方法上有三层分析。

第一层是损失分解。作者把短答案 QA 的 F1 拆成 precision、recall 和 answer length，发现减 experts 后，Qwen3、DeepSeek、Qwen1.5 三个家族的主要损失来自 answer form：模型通常仍能找到正确答案，但更容易输出冗长、不够“只给短答案”的形式。这个分解很关键，因为如果只看 F1，很容易误判成知识检索或推理能力下降。

第二层是定位损失来源。作者做 token-level 和 span-level intervention：在 uniform reduction 的基础上，只让某个 token 或某段 span 保持 full experts，观察能恢复多少 F1 / answer NLL。结果显示，损失高度集中在 prompt 的少数位置。Qwen3 的关键位置是答案前两个模板 token，而且不是某个固定模板词本身，而是“紧邻答案前的接口位置”；DeepSeek-V2-Lite 的关键位置在 prompt 开头 8–32 个 token，对应标准模板里承载 instruction 的部分；Qwen1.5-MoE 则偏向最后两个 token。作者还做了 family-level 对比，说明这种敏感位置与各家 chat layout 有关，而不是统一规律。

第三层是机制分析。作者用 attention-path blocking 和 layer repair 检查 Qwen3 中这些关键 token 是否承担了把 instruction 传到答案位置的路径。结论是证据与“instruction transport”一致：这些 token 像是任务接口，负责把“只回答短答案”这样的格式约束送到生成起点。作者还预注册式地测量了几个常见猜想是否成立，包括 first-token attention sink、activation norm / largest activation、super experts 等。结果显示，减 experts 后第一 token 的 attention mass、norm、最大激活变化都不到 4%，恢复这些统计也解释不了大部分质量损失；super experts 和 massive activations 也不足以解释主要现象。也就是说，问题不太像是全局表示崩坏，更像是少数关键路径上的路由容量不足。2c:T861,The setup is uniform prefill expert reduction. For prompt tokens only, each MoE layer reduces routed experts from K to k', while generated tokens still use the full K and shared experts are never reduced. Qwen3-30B-A3B is the main model, typically with 8→4 reduction; single-token interventions use 8→2 to amplify measurable effects.

The analysis has three layers. First, the authors decompose the quality drop. They split short-answer F1 into precision, recall, and answer length. In Qwen3, DeepSeek, and Qwen1.5, the main loss comes from answer form: the model usually still retrieves the right content, but becomes less compliant with the “short answer only” format. This is important because raw F1 alone could be misread as a knowledge or reasoning failure.

Second, they localize where the loss is made. Using token-level and span-level interventions, they keep only selected prompt positions at full experts on top of a uniformly reduced baseline and measure how much F1 or answer NLL is recovered. The damage is highly concentrated. In Qwen3, the critical positions are the two tokens immediately before the answer; in DeepSeek-V2-Lite, the first 8–32 prompt tokens matter most, matching the instruction-bearing region of its standard layout; in Qwen1.5-MoE, the last two prompt tokens matter most. For Qwen3, the effect follows whichever tokens precede the answer, rather than a specific template token identity.

Third, they probe mechanism. Attention-path blocking and layer-repair experiments in Qwen3 are consistent with an instruction-transport account: these interface tokens seem to carry the short-answer constraint to the answer position. The paper also directly tests several alternative explanations, including first-token attention sinks, activation norms, largest activations, and super experts. Those measurements do not explain most of the loss. The first-token sink statistics change by less than 4%, and restoring those statistics does not recover the main quality drop. So the phenomenon looks less like a global representational collapse and more like insufficient routing capacity on a few critical paths.2d:T73c,The main task is HotpotQA in the distractor setting, with prompts of about 1,800 tokens: an instruction to give only the short answer, two demonstrations, ten paragraphs, and the question. Evaluation uses greedy decoding and F1. The main model is Qwen3-30B-A3B with reasoning disabled; additional families are DeepSeek-V2-Lite-Chat, Qwen1.5-MoE-A2.7B-Chat, and OLMoE-1B-7B-Instruct.

On Qwen3, the unmodified model scores F1 69.81 on a 2,000-question run. Uniform 8→4 reduction costs about 3.34–3.38 F1 depending on the batch. The decomposition shows that this is driven mostly by answer length / form rather than a large recall collapse. Single-token experiments use 500 questions; span and family comparisons use 2,000-question runs.

For localization, the key positions explain a substantial fraction of the damage. In Qwen3, restoring full experts at the two tokens before the answer accounts for roughly half of the answer-likelihood loss. In DeepSeek-V2-Lite, the first 8–32 prompt tokens account for about one fifth. The paper is careful to note that in answer NLL these positions explain only part of the total damage, so there are still more diffuse secondary effects.

The equal-budget experiments on real prompts are also useful. With a fixed number of full-expert prompt tokens, placing them on the task interface helps much more than scattering them randomly in the documents. In the tested templates, simple “keep the ends” rules are not clearly worse than more explicit marking schemes, so the result is better read as a placement principle than as a new selector. Another positive detail is experimental discipline: some batches had prespecified primary comparisons and decision rules written into the analysis scripts before the formal run, and the paper clearly separates prespecified from exploratory and post hoc analyses.2e:T7d5,The method has three key components. First, the architecture. HydraLLM uses an MQA backbone and treats the number of active query heads k as the single runtime width-control variable. Embedding width, attention projections, FFN width, and output-head width all scale linearly with k/Hq, and each subnetwork always takes a prefix of the corresponding dimensions. This creates a strictly nested family of subnetworks inside one parameter set. MQA is a deliberate choice: shared KV heads make head-aligned slicing cleaner than in standard multi-head attention.

Second, offline importance-based reordering. A pretrained static checkpoint does not satisfy the monotonicity needed by prefix slicing. Important units may be scattered across channels. The authors therefore collect activation statistics on a calibration corpus, score unit importance, and permute sliceable dimensions so that more important embedding channels, attention units, FFN neurons, and output channels move toward the front. They compare activation magnitude, VxC, and CETT. Their conclusion is that CETT is better aligned with prefix slicing because it measures normalized contribution to component outputs rather than raw activation strength. The permutation must be propagated consistently across linked tensors so that the model function is preserved.

Third, stochastic subnetwork training. After reordering, they adapt the checkpoint by sampling one width k per step and updating only the active subnetwork. Earlier prefix units are active under more sampled widths, so they naturally receive more gradient updates. This reinforces the importance ordering and helps shared parameters support all budgets. The training objective remains standard language modeling; the novelty is in the conversion and training schedule rather than in a new loss. For LVLMs, they add an elastic projection module that maps visual features into text embedding spaces of varying widths, so the same slicing principle extends to multimodal generation.2f:T672,The experiments support three main claims. First, medium-width subnetworks remain competitive. On TriviaQA, the 75%-head HydraLLM subnetwork improves accuracy by 2.2 points over a baseline configuration that still has 17.25% more parameters. That suggests the converted subnetwork is not just a degraded pruned model.

Second, reordering is critical at small widths. At 25% active heads, importance-based reordering improves post-training perplexity by 16.2%. This is one of the most informative numbers in the paper, because it shows that the bottleneck is not only adaptation training; the checkpoint must first be reorganized for prefix slicing to make sense.

Third, the runtime gains are real. The 25% configuration increases token-generation throughput by 75.29% relative to the full model, so the method yields actual inference savings rather than only parameter-count reductions.

For multimodal experiments, the paper extends the same idea to LVLMs and reports a CLIPScore of 66.9 at 50% active heads, above the 65.3 score of Gemma 3 4B, which is described as roughly 7x larger. This indicates that the elastic projection does not destroy visual-text alignment. From a foundation-model perspective, though, CLIPScore alone is not enough; broader VLM evaluation would be needed to judge robustness.

The most useful ablations are the comparisons among importance metrics, the effect of reordering versus no reordering, and the behavior across 25%/50%/75% widths. The paper’s main empirical message is that small-subnetwork quality is driven mostly by the quality of reordering and by whether adaptation training covers the width range well.30:T781,The framework is sparse collaborative decoding. A draft model generates most tokens, and a stronger target model is invoked only at selected intervention points to generate a bounded corrective segment. That segment is appended to the visible context, and the draft resumes generation. The intervention is purely textual: no logits, hidden states, or parameters are shared. This makes the method compatible with black-box targets and clearly different from speculative decoding, which keeps target computation tightly coupled to generation.

The key technical question is the trigger policy. The paper first tests the most obvious signals: current-token entropy, negative log-likelihood, logit margin, top-k mass, and short-window velocity features. These turn out to be weak predictors of final correctness. The reason is intuitive: a model can be confidently wrong on a bad reasoning path, or briefly uncertain on a path that is still recoverable.

The authors then move to trajectory-level failure prediction. Instead of using a single token state, they summarize the confidence trajectory observed so far. Features are built from time series of entropy, NLL, margin, probability concentration, and temporal variation, then aggregated into means, extrema, percentiles, slopes, variances, and position-dependent summaries. A lightweight logistic predictor is trained using only draft correctness labels. At inference time, the system updates these trajectory features online and triggers the target when predicted failure risk crosses a threshold.

This creates an explicit trigger-timing tradeoff. Early calls have more leverage to redirect the trajectory but weaker evidence; late calls have stronger evidence but less room to help. The paper evaluates this learned online policy against fixed-position open-loop schedules, whole-example cascade routing, posthoc rewriting, self-consistency, and speculative decoding.31:T56d,Three results matter most. First, local uncertainty is nearly useless as a trigger. On GSM8K with Qwen-1.5B as the draft model, entropy and NLL achieve only AUC 0.531 and 0.528 for predicting final failure, and velocity features are similarly weak. This is an important negative result: common token-level confidence signals do not reliably indicate when a reasoning trajectory is going off track.

Second, trajectory-level summaries are much stronger. A lightweight logistic predictor over aggregate confidence-path features reaches AUC 0.86. That gap is large enough to support the paper’s main claim that trajectory shape carries information that local uncertainty misses.

Third, the learned trigger improves actual collaborative decoding. Across five GSM8K draft-target pairs, learned triggering improves final accuracy by 5.3 to 20.0 points over the best open-loop sparse-collaboration schedule. The method also transfers to StrategyQA and shows gains on MATH-500, although the paper is careful to note that benefits depend on alignment between early failure prediction and the target model’s ability to repair the specific failure mode.

The evaluation also reports target calls, target-generated tokens, wall-clock time when available, rescue rate, and break rate. These metrics are useful because they separate genuine efficiency gains from simple overuse of the target model.32:T43c,For a skim, focus on two parts: the table showing that local uncertainty features have near-chance AUC, and the table showing the much stronger trajectory-feature predictor plus the online triggering results. The most important sections are the introduction, problem setup, trajectory-level failure prediction, and the main results. The paper is less about model internals and more about how observable draft states are summarized and how intervention timing is analyzed.

A 25-35 minute read is enough to capture the main idea. If you want to inspect the method more carefully, look at three issues: how failure labels are defined for training the trigger; how the maximum corrective-segment length affects rescue rate versus break rate; and whether the comparison to speculative decoding is fully apples-to-apples, since the two methods optimize different objectives. The main takeaway is not the logistic regressor itself, but the broader empirical point: in multi-step reasoning, failures are often trajectory-level phenomena, and single-token confidence is a weak control signal.33:T409,Harness-Delta Attribution (HDA) decomposes the performance difference between (M, H0) and (M, Ĥ) into:
1) Artifact-driven overfitting: estimate a detectable lower bound by evaluating an artifact-neutralized version of the evolved harness, where shortcut-exploiting components (hardcoded patterns, dataset-format hacks) are removed or disabled.
2) Test-time scaling: construct a compute-matched baseline harness that uses the same inference-time budget as the evolved harness (e.g., number of samples, voting/reranking, tool-call budget), and measure how much of the gain is reproducible purely by spending more compute.
3) Residual: the remaining improvement after subtracting (1) and (2), interpreted as an upper bound on generalizable harness improvement (upper bound because some overfitting may remain undetected).

A key point is that held-out evaluation alone can still overstate generalization when search/test splits share benchmark artifacts; HDA adds compute matching and artifact neutralization as orthogonal diagnostics.34:T551,The key move is to decompose identifier hints into two orthogonal design choices. Identifier selection decides which program entities are revealed: edited files, new functions, classes, attributes, and so on. Identifier projection decides how much of each selected entity is exposed: name, signature, location, semantic description, behavioral guidance, etc. Existing benchmark hint schemes often entangle these choices, making comparisons hard to interpret.

Within this framework, the paper proposes DefUse for selection and Declaration for projection. DefUse targets identifiers that sit on the interaction boundary between implementation and tests, rather than disclosing every new component in the reference solution. This is a narrower objective: remove hidden interface mismatch, not reveal internal design. Declaration then exposes declaration-level attributes of those entities, such as names, signatures, and locations, while avoiding semantic descriptions or implementation hints that would move closer to the reference design.

The paper also introduces an LLM-based assessment layer to score whether a hint attribute is necessary for task resolution, whether it discloses reference-solution information, and whether it guides behavior beyond interface clarification. That judge is used as a complement to execution-based success, not a replacement.35:T42f,The experiments span five repository-level coding benchmarks across Python and Java, evaluated with three language models. The paper’s emphasis is not on a single best score, but on controlled comparisons between selection policies and projection policies.

The main empirical finding is that higher solve rates can coincide with greater disclosure of implementation choices. In other words, some hint schemes improve benchmark performance not merely by removing unfair ambiguity, but by leaking parts of the solution. The controlled setup shows that both axes matter: which identifiers are selected and which attributes of those identifiers are projected can each materially change measured task resolution.

Attribute ablations further separate the effects of names, signatures, locations, and semantic descriptions. On SWE-bench Pro, the LLM-based analysis is used to distinguish necessary interface information from spoiler-like content. The practical takeaway is that benchmark quality cannot be judged from pass rate alone; it needs a disclosure analysis as well.36:T534,The methodology is a controlled ablation where the MCP gateway is the primary independent variable.
- Gateway families:
  - Flat gateways expose the full tool list to the model upfront.
  - Hierarchical gateways expose meta-tools for tool discovery, letting the agent retrieve a subset on demand.
- Benchmark construction:
  - Built on two existing agent evaluation assets: DecodingTrust-Agent Platform (DTap) and MCP-Universe.
  - Adds a large curated pool of task-irrelevant “noisy tools” to simulate enterprise-scale tool registries.
- Tool scaling protocol:
  - A real-world tool pool of 11,118 tools across 251 MCP servers.
  - Up to 1,500 irrelevant tools are injected per evaluation setting to trace scaling curves.
- Evaluation matrix:
  - 7 gateway implementations (6 open-source + 1 closed-source).
  - 3 agent configurations combining two model tiers (GPT-5.1, GPT-5.4) and two harnesses (OpenAI Agents SDK, OpenClaw).
- Research questions and metrics align to four axes: success vs tool count, efficiency vs tool count, robustness to red-teaming attacks, and modulation by model/harness.
- Key design choice: using a real tool pool plus controlled noise injection (instead of purely synthetic tools) to keep tool descriptions and distributions realistic, while making tool-surface size an explicit, tunable variable.37:T5a1,The provided full-text excerpt contains strong qualitative findings but not enough table/figure numbers to quote exact deltas:
- Asymmetric sensitivity to tool-surface scaling:
  - Flat gateways show mild but consistent success degradation as more noisy tools are injected.
  - Hierarchical gateways are largely invariant to the number of injected tools.
  - The crossover depends on model strength: weaker models may still prefer flat gateways overall; stronger models can benefit from hierarchical gateways once noise is large.
- Context-dependent efficiency trade-off:
  - With few noisy tools, flat gateways reduce turns and latency.
  - With many noisy tools, flat gateways incur near-linear growth in token usage and context-processing latency; hierarchical gateways keep token usage closer to tool-count-invariant (driven by discovery calls and returned subset size).
- Security evaluation:
  - Both direct prompt injection and indirect tool/environment injection are tested; gateway architecture changes the effective attack surface and success rates.
- System-stack effects:
  - Model tier and harness choice shift the success–cost–security frontier, arguing against evaluations that fix only the model.
Note: for exact numeric results (success %, token counts, latency, attack success rates), consult the main result tables and scaling curves (e.g., the figures referenced around the RQs). They are not included in the excerpt.38:T62c,The setup is fairly clean. The authors start from 8 tasks in MLE-bench Lite, each with a baseline solution chosen near the middle of the leaderboard so there is room to improve or degrade. An LLM proposes a modification, implements it, runs training, and obtains the actual score. This process is expanded into a small tree: 4 ideas from the baseline, then 2 follow-up ideas from each resulting node, giving 12 ideas per tree. Three models act as proposer-implementers: GPT-6 Astra, Claude Fable 5.1, and Kimi K3. Across 8 tasks and 3 proposer models, this yields 288 ideas, with 269 retained after failed runs and unusable predictions are removed. Because the tasks use different metrics and scales, the paper normalizes each realized score change by the within-task standard deviation of realized changes, and flips signs so positive always means improvement. Predicted changes are normalized the same way. This allows pooling across log-loss, ROC-AUC, and RMSE tasks. The evaluation then looks at numerical error, correlation, directional accuracy, the effect of more reasoning or more context, the effect of the implementing model, and self-preference. A particularly good design choice is the improve/worsen prompting split: half the ideas are intended to improve and half to worsen, not to create trivial bad examples, but to avoid a one-sided outcome distribution concentrated near small positive gains. Compared with prior work on pairwise selection, this is closer to real experiment management, where one needs an expected-value estimate rather than a binary preference.39:T561,The dataset is modest in size but informative in structure: 269 executed ideas, 8 tasks, and 5 predictor models. The main result is that current models have limited ability to predict numerical outcomes of ML experiments. They appear to do better than naive guessing and can extract some weak signal about which ideas are more promising, but the errors and calibration issues are large enough that they are not reliable stand-alone experiment filters. The ablations are useful. Increasing reasoning level or adding more prompt context does not produce a robust improvement, which suggests the bottleneck is not simply insufficient deliberation or missing local context. It looks more like the models lack an internalized model of training dynamics and empirical payoff. The identity of the implementing model matters for realized outcomes, but predictor models only partially account for that implementation variance. The paper also probes self-preference, asking whether models are more optimistic about ideas from their own family; this seems secondary rather than the main story. The most useful takeaway is not a single headline metric but the error pattern: models often generate plausible mechanistic explanations for why an idea should help, yet they lack a stable scale for expected gain and struggle with changes that sound reasonable but have little empirical effect.3a:T6a4,The methodology is carefully controlled. The authors introduce JUPE, a VQA-style benchmark where each item fixes the image, question, candidate answer, and correctness label, while varying only the explanation condition. This isolates the causal effect of explanation text on judgment. Judges output one of four ordinal labels—definitely incorrect, probably incorrect, probably correct, definitely correct—which are also collapsed into reject versus accept for binary analyses. To reduce prompt artifacts, the option order is randomized, the model answers with A–D choices, and the prompt uses an image-first layout. The benchmark spans three visually grounded domains: medical VQA, chart reasoning, and hallucination-prone visual questions, drawing from VQA-RAD, PMC-VQA, ChartQA, ChartQAPro, HaloQuest, and PRE-HAL. The main set contains about 180 queries, plus a 70-item induced-wrong easy set built from questions that profiling models usually solve correctly and then paired with intentionally wrong answers. This is a strong design choice because it tests whether explanations can flip judgments even on items that are otherwise easy. Explanation conditions include jointly generated chain-of-thought rationales, plain post-hoc justifications, and persuasion-oriented arguments built using 41 persuasion techniques. A key analytical move is the use of signal detection theory. Rather than relying on accuracy alone, the paper separates shifts in decision criterion from changes in discriminability. That matters because a judge can become more accepting of both correct and incorrect answers, leaving overall accuracy nearly unchanged while substantially weakening wrong-answer rejection.3b:T5d0,The evaluation covers 14 MLLM judges from 5 model families. The main finding is consistent: ordinary plausible explanations are enough to materially reduce rejection of incorrect answers. On average, plain justifications reduce the rejection rate of wrong answers by 12.7 percentage points, chain-of-thought explanations by 13.8 points, and explicitly persuasive explanations by 12.5 points. The similarity across these conditions is important because it shows the problem does not require specialized persuasion attacks; normal-looking explanations already trigger it. A stronger result is that among wrong answers judged definitely incorrect without explanations, 36% to 39% become accepted once a plausible explanation is added. The signal-detection analysis indicates that the dominant effect is a shift in decision criterion toward acceptance, not a clear mean improvement in discriminability. In other words, the judges are not becoming better at reading the image; they are becoming more willing to trust the answer. The paper also checks robustness across domains, an out-of-distribution grounding set, different explanation generators, image degradation settings, and rating protocols. Common mitigations such as caution prompting and solve-first verification show limited or inconsistent gains. For anyone using judge models in training loops, the practical implication is straightforward: answer-plus-explanation formats can systematically inflate pass rates for wrong outputs.3c:T4e0,The framework defines desired agent behavior as a set of situational rules: if the current situation satisfies condition P, the agent should follow criterion Q. During evaluation, an LLM interlocutor interactively advances the conversation by uniformly sampling situations from a predefined action space. This is the main design choice. Instead of waiting for rare situations to appear naturally, the benchmark forces balanced coverage over the situation space.

A context-aware LLM judge then evaluates each agent response only against the subset of rules that apply at that turn. The output is therefore rule-wise and turn-wise, not a single opaque score for the whole dialogue. To improve judge reliability, the authors validate the judge on human-verified synthetic golden dialogues before using it in the benchmark.

The demonstration domain is Socratic math tutoring. The paper defines 12 expert-authored pedagogical rules spanning seven categories, including Socratic questioning, ambiguity clarification, and error handling. The key methodological choice is to replace reference responses with explicit behavioral rules. That is sensible for open-ended dialogue, where many responses can be acceptable if they satisfy the right constraints.3d:T8c5,方法核心是 Bounded Continuation Analysis（BCA）。作者把给定 prompt 下的自回归生成过程近似成一个有界的随机过程：从初始前缀出发，逐步展开一个 bounded continuation tree，只保留有限深度、且在概率上足够重要的续写路径。树上的节点是 token prefix，边对应下一 token 的条件概率。由于完整枚举整个输出空间不可行，BCA 只在有限 horizon 内构造高概率子树，再把终止续写交给语义 harm evaluator 打标签，判断该续写是否构成有害响应。随后，作者计算在这个有界树中“最终到达有害终端”的总概率，称为 bounded harm reachability。

这个设计有几个关键点。第一，它不是把 harm 判定放在 token 级，而是放在语义级终端续写上，这样能避免局部 token 模式和真实有害行为之间的错位。第二，它显式利用了自回归模型的条件概率，因此得到的是一个 reachability probability，而不是 judge 打分的启发式分数。第三，它把问题表述成 probabilistic model checking 里的 reachability 问题，用离散时间马尔可夫链和 PCTL 风格的“eventually reach harmful state”来组织计算，这让指标定义比较干净。

在固定 prompt 之外，作者还定义了 GAS（generative attack susceptibility），把自适应攻击预算映射到一个概率值：在攻击者迄今找到的所有 prompt 中，哪一个 prompt 的 bounded harm reachability 最高。这个定义适合黑盒 adaptive jailbreak search。实验里主要结合 PAIR 和 TAP 两类搜索：前者是顺序式 prompt refinement，后者是带分支和剪枝的搜索。作者还讨论了把 BCA 作为搜索信号，与传统 judge-guided 搜索并行使用，从而覆盖不同失败模式。

从方法取舍看，作者没有试图估计“全空间真实 harmful probability”，而是退到 bounded、high-probability tree 上做近似。这是合理的，因为完整分布不可算，且安全评测更关心攻击者在有限生成预算内能否触达有害区域。代价是结果依赖 horizon、树展开策略和 evaluator 质量；作者也承认 BCA 是一个 bounded proxy，而不是精确的全分布积分。3e:T84b,The core method is Bounded Continuation Analysis (BCA). For a given prompt, the model’s autoregressive generation process is approximated as a bounded stochastic process over token prefixes. Starting from the prompt, BCA expands a bounded continuation tree up to a finite horizon, keeping only continuations that are sufficiently probable. Nodes are token prefixes, edges are next-token conditional probabilities, and terminal continuations are labeled as harmful or safe by a semantic harm evaluator. BCA then computes the probability of eventually reaching a harmful terminal within this bounded tree, which the paper calls bounded harm reachability.

Several design choices matter. First, harm is evaluated semantically at terminal continuations rather than by token-level heuristics, which better matches the actual safety question. Second, the score is probability-aware: it uses the model’s conditional distribution instead of only sampled outputs.
